Alex Karp on Why AI Will Hurt the Middle Class: You Can’t Have Billionaires Becoming 100x Wealthier

The Builders with Walter Isaacson(30.8K)·43:05·2026-11-10
Alex KarpCEO, Palantir Walter IsaacsonHost, The Builders
TL;DRAlex Karp argues that unchecked AI and massive wealth concentration will widen class divides, threatening American democracy unless tech, government, and society realign to protect human dignity, civil liberties, and the working class.
Dyslexia as a Cognitive Advantage: Alex Karp attributes his ability to build unconventional software to his dyslexia, defining it as a non-one-to-one relationship with written language taxonomy. Being dyslexic prevented him from being "colonized by the broader culture,"...

TL;DR

Alex Karp argues that unchecked AI and massive wealth concentration will widen class divides, threatening American democracy unless tech, government, and society realign to protect human dignity, civil liberties, and the working class.

The Outsider Mindset & Palantir's Origins

  • Dyslexia as a Cognitive Advantage:
    • Alex Karp attributes his ability to build unconventional software to his dyslexia, defining it as a non-one-to-one relationship with written language taxonomy.
    • Being dyslexic prevented him from being "colonized by the broader culture," allowing him to interact with technology and real-world problems objectively.
  • Rejecting Silicon Valley Norms:
    • Palantir operated for roughly three years without revenue to build a high-value anti-terror product rather than a simple, addictive consumer app.
    • Karp spent years studying Martin Heidegger's philosophy and its influence on modern regressive political movements before co-founding Palantir with Peter Thiel.
  • The Genesis of Palantir:
    • Inspired by PayPal's fraud-identification software, Peter Thiel approached Karp during their time at Stanford Law School to bring similar data-connecting methodologies to the intelligence space.

Surveillance, National Security, and Privacy

  • Balancing Security and Civil Liberties:
    • Palantir’s products are utilized in Europe and the U.S. to dismantle organized criminal gangs, stop border-enforcement trafficking, and prevent terrorist attacks under strict legal mandates and warrants.
    • Karp warns that failing to stop these threats would give rise to far-left or far-right fascism, resulting in total domestic surveillance.
  • Commercial vs. Government Overreach:
    • Karp argues that citizens over-index on fearing government surveillance while ignoring commercial data collection by tech companies that heavily influence human thought and opinions.
    • He advocates for strict legislation to restrict battlefield AI technologies from being deployed domestically.

Wealth Disparity, Capitalism, and the Middle Class

  • The Dangerous Fracturing of Society:
    • Karp asserts that billionaires becoming 100x wealthier while the middle class and working class see marginal gains breaks the tacit covenant between wealth creation and societal value.
    • AI will exacerbate these disparities, creating extreme wealth for tech elites while leaving working-class vocational workers behind.
  • Flaws of Proposed Solutions:
    • A traditional wealth tax is impractical, fails to help the poor, breaks tech culture liquidity, and inherently requires invasive surveillance to assess asset values.
    • Karp's Taxation Compromise: Proposes raising taxes on the wealthy *only* under the condition that every unit of tax raised is matched by half a unit of identified government waste, directly funding programs that improve working-class lives without destroying the tech ecosystem.

Silicon Valley, National Sovereignty, and Enterprise AI

  • The Broken Social Contract:
    • Silicon Valley is described as a "highly insulated island" that has lost touch with mainstream America, producing ads and consumerism while generating anti-government sentiment.
    • Despite this, Karp notes that the U.S. military—the only indisputably noble institution in the eyes of most Americans—remains the foundational hybrid of government, Stanford academia, and venture capital that birthed the valley.
  • Enterprise Backlash Against LLMs:
    • U.S. enterprises are enraged and apoplectic because they are paying high fees for Large Language Models while unknowingly having their proprietary data used to train models that ultimately compete against them.
    • Palantir combats this by providing "ontologies"—contextual frameworks that act as an alpha creator and a protective layer, allowing enterprises to interact with open and closed AI models safely and securely.

What It Means to Be Human

  • The Challenge of Artificial Superintelligence:
    • As AI surpasses human cognitive abilities—long considered the core capacity underlying moral systems and claims of superiority—society faces the question of what makes humans special.
  • The Radical Protection of the Constitution:
    • Karp emphasizes that the U.S. Constitution uniquely frames rights as granted by a higher figure, rooting human value in something above and beyond mere cognitive skill.
    • This foundational belief protects the artistic, individual, and particular nature of humanity, making the defense of American constitutional order vital in the age of intelligent machines.

Notable Quotes

"You can't have people becoming a hundred times wealthier when they're already billionaires and everyone else is becoming 5% wealthier, okay? So that's legitimate." — Alex Karp
"The valley was built as a hybrid basically university, government, capital." — Alex Karp

Amazon AWS CEO: AI boom is just getting started

Sources Podcast(315.0K)·52:51·2026-11-10
Matt GarmanCEO, AWS
TL;DRAWS CEO Matt Garman explains how the AI boom is accelerating Amazon's cloud business toward a potential $1 trillion revenue run rate by driving core on-premise workloads into the cloud.
Despite adding one GPU every 14 seconds, AWS anticipates severe capacity shortages for the next couple of years. Accelerating On-Premise Migration: AI agents require access to all enterprise data sources, forcing companies to migrate legacy workloads (mainframes, VMware...

TL;DR

AWS CEO Matt Garman explains how the AI boom is accelerating Amazon's cloud business toward a potential $1 trillion revenue run rate by driving core on-premise workloads into the cloud. Despite adding one GPU every 14 seconds, AWS anticipates severe capacity shortages for the next couple of years.

AWS Growth & The AI-Driven Cloud Migration

  • Accelerating On-Premise Migration:
    • AI agents require access to all enterprise data sources, forcing companies to migrate legacy workloads (mainframes, VMware clusters, on-prem databases) into the cloud.
    • AWS revenue has accelerated for six consecutive quarters, currently standing at an $182 billion run rate growing at 39% year-over-year.
  • Broad Customer Base:
    • Growth spans across the global top 5,000 enterprises, governments, and a rapidly expanding long tail of startups.
    • Building a two-person startup has never been easier, with companies scaling rapidly using cloud infrastructure and AI amplification.

Compute Economics, Scaling, and Capacity Constraints

  • Inference Scale:
    • Inference represents the vast majority of AWS's AI workloads and is described as being "below 1% of its eventual scale."
    • Every time inference gets 50% cheaper, usage increases by an order of magnitude.
  • Cost Management Controls:
    • AWS is investing in robust cost management tools (ranging from account shut-off limits to real-time budget alerts and model-switching controls) to help companies avoid "scaling into bankruptcy."
  • Localized vs. Systemic Risk:
    • Garman views market risk as localized to heavily debt-financed NeoClouds with high customer/workload concentration (mostly large training clusters) rather than systemic for AWS, which distributes its compute across millions of diverse enterprise and startup customers.
  • Staged Capital Allocation:
    • AWS stages its capital expenditures—purchasing long-lead items like land and power contracts years in advance while deferring server purchases to the last possible minute to maintain option value.

Data Centers, Energy, and Community Relations

  • Community Education & Citizenship:
    • AWS is actively working to separate responsible data center operators from those who bypass regulations.
    • AWS brings power and pays for grid usage, reduces local tax loads, and is the single largest creator of new carbon-zero energy sources globally over the last 5 years.
  • Water Usage & Efficiency:
    • AWS is committed to being water positive by 2030 (returning more water to communities than it uses).
    • The company's largest data centers today use less water than 40 US households (less than a single city block).

Future Infrastructure: Space, Oceans, and Deserts

  • Space Data Centers: Considered highly unlikely to be economically feasible or practical in the next decade or two due to infrequent/expensive rocket launches, high thermal dissipation challenges in a vacuum, and intense radiation.
  • Ocean Data Centers: Dismissed as impractical due to cooling and structural movement issues.
  • Deserts: Highlighted as the most promising alternative for future data center deployment, offering environmental heat sinks, solar energy potential, and compatibility with liquid cooling.

The Cloud Stack, Chips, and AI Models

  • Full-Stack Strategy:
    • AWS competes across all layers of the cloud stack—from raw infrastructure and custom silicon (Trainium, Inferentia) to managed services, databases, and end-user applications.
  • Tranium External Availability:
    • AWS is beginning to sell Tranium chips externally (similar to Google with TPUs) to capture the 85% of workloads that still reside on-premises and satisfy sovereign AI demands.
  • Frontier Model Strategy (Nova):
    • Garman confirmed that recent team changes and model sunsetting were a "reset" rather than a retreat; AWS remains fully invested in developing first-party frontier models.
  • Agent Safety & Automated Reasoning:
    • AWS is heavily investing in "automated reasoning" (applying mathematical proofs to computer science) to build deterministic guardrails for non-deterministic AI agents, targeting a deployment timeline starting next year.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "I anticipate that we'll be short of capacity for the next couple of years." — Matt Garman
  • "By 2030 we'll be water positive where we bring more water to every community that we operate in than than we use." — Matt Garman
  • Space-based data centers are "highly unlikely to be economically feasible or practical in the next decade or two." — Matt Garman
  • Automated reasoning guardrails for AI agents will see initial deployments "next year." — Matt Garman

Notable Quotes

"If their core workloads aren't in a cloud environment... they just have a hard time having agents talk to those pieces, pulling data from all those different data stores doesn't work. And so we've actually seen is customers... really accelerated when people realized they weren't going to get the benefit of AI if they didn't get those workloads in the cloud." — Matt Garman
"We're of this fortunate position... We spread that usage across you know we have tens of hundreds of thousands millions of customers that are all using AWS... And fortunately or unfortunately, but right now the demand is far outstripping the supply." — Matt Garman
"Technology is so important to our economy today that we can't just turn it off and decide to not have it. I don't think that that would be good for our country." — Matt Garman

But that's on us to co-invent what those look like. I actually think one of the big technology unlocks that I feel really excited about is it's largely called neuro-symbolic AI, but effectively it's this field where actually Amazon has been probably the single biggest investment that we make internally in research is around what we call automated reasoning. It's applying mathematical proofs to computer science problems to prove things to be true. And so a tangible example of that is when you go and create a new permissions model in AWS, you run it through IAM access analyzer, and it basically looks at the whole possibility of all of the permissions that you have inside of your org and it can tell you is this going to conflict with some other permission, right? Like this one says you should have access, this one doesn't, and it tells you those conflict, you better go figure that out or we won't accept it. And so if you just brute force try to go figure those things out, it's like more computers than the earth has to go figure out that calculation, but through mathematical proof you can actually do it relatively fast and get to 100% proof that these things come out. That's right, x + y = y + x. If you just brute force try to prove that, you of course can't because there's an infinite number of combinations of that thing, but math can prove it in like, you know, three steps or whatever it is, right? And so if you can take that and you can basically say I want a non-deterministic agent that's going to be able to go out and try to process an insurance claim, right? I want it to go figure things out. Like it's hard, it's going to go out and look at some data sources and try different things, but I want to make sure that it never breaks this rule or this rule or this rule or follows the policies that I have. If you could have an automated reasoning agent that basically helps steer and build guardrails to your kind of core AI agent that basically could deterministically prove that it would never break invariants, I think you could actually build a set of tools there by combining automated reasoning with traditional AI systems that could give provably correct agents that would follow rules. And so that is an area that we're investing a lot in, and it's showing a ton of promise, and it's still early, but tools like that can be really powerful. And I think it's one of the things that could unlock where you can imagine an insurance company saying, wow, I would love to just in a fully automated way let my agent process insurance claims. Now it's like people could submit insurance claims, they get an answer back in 10 seconds. Like it would be game changing as opposed to wait a month to figure out if you get a claim from your insurance agent because they'd be comfortable sending those things off in a fully automated way. It's where actually the experience gets better for customers, there's probably more business that they can do, but today they're not comfortable because you actually have to have a human in the loop. Fireside | John Collison and Sam Gerstenzang on building companies with AI

Stripe(121.0K)·28:42·428 views·2026-11-10
John CollisonCofounder, Stripe Sam GerstenzangCofounder, Boulton & Watt
TL;DRBoulton & Watt incubator co-founder Sam Gerstenzang and Stripe co-founder John Collison discuss building vertical SaaS companies by working backwards from a business's P&L, using AI coding to build customized software for every user, and leveraging scale and risk-taking to compete with private equity rollups.
The "Slow" Strategy: Sam Gerstenzang explains that Boulton & Watt starts only one company every few years, dedicating 100% of focus to it until it is past product-market fit before bringing in a replacement CEO. Target Industries: They hunt for businesses that are overlooked,...

TL;DR

Boulton & Watt incubator co-founder Sam Gerstenzang and Stripe co-founder John Collison discuss building vertical SaaS companies by working backwards from a business's P&L, using AI coding to build customized software for every user, and leveraging scale and risk-taking to compete with private equity rollups.

The Boulton & Watt Incubator Model

  • The "Slow" Strategy: Sam Gerstenzang explains that Boulton & Watt starts only one company every few years, dedicating 100% of focus to it until it is past product-market fit before bringing in a replacement CEO.
  • Target Industries: They hunt for businesses that are overlooked, regulated, complex, taboo, or local with geographic monopolies (e.g., med spas, funeral homes, freight brokerages).
  • P&L Inversion: Instead of starting with a standalone product, they work backwards from the P&L—identifying where businesses make and spend money, and aiming to become the overarching infrastructure (Moxy, taxes, and real estate).
  • Company 1 (Moxy): Sam Gerstenzang states Moxy helps doctors and nurses open med spas (Botox and laser shops), solving state legal licensing (similar to Stripe Atlas), providing marketing services, software, and bulk pharma purchasing.
  • Company 2 (Funeral Homes): Operates as the end-to-end operating system for independent funeral homes.
  • Company 3 (Freight Brokerage): Sam Gerstenzang notes they empower individual freight brokers (often working with 1-10 trucks) to leave big firms, take on risk independently, and compete with PE-backed rollups.

The Impact of AI on Software Development

  • Custom Software Per User: Sam Gerstenzang notes that post-AI coding, instead of using a single standardized product, they now rapidly build custom software for individual customers (e.g., building a custom automated portal for a freight broker in two hours).
  • The 1:1 PM-to-Engineer Ratio: Sam Gerstenzang highlights that post-AI, Boulton & Watt maintains a 1:1 product manager-to-engineer ratio (compared to traditional 1:6 or 1:8 ratios), keeping engineering headcount flat while radically increasing surface area and deployment speed.
  • Architecture & Refactoring: Sam Gerstenzang notes they had to refactor older codebases (dating back 4 years) so that AI tools can understand them as well as humans. All customer-facing code still undergoes manual software engineer review to prevent maintainable "slop."
  • AI Limitations: Sam Gerstenzang notes that AI still struggles with synthesis (e.g., generating financial forecasts or creating new business ideas) and high-stakes automation where users have zero tolerance for mistakes (e.g., front-desk med spa phone calls).

Taking Risk as a Core Business Competency

  • Risk Brokerage: Sam Gerstenzang argues that pure software businesses must evolve because software gets commoditized. Boulton & Watt's core competitive advantage is taking on real-world risk for customers (e.g., HIPPA compliance, credit risk, shipper/broker liabilities).
  • Systems of Record vs. Workflow: John Collison and Sam Gerstenzang discuss how systems of record (like Salesforce, Workday, or Toast) must reach deeper into solving end-to-end business problems and risk management to survive against cheaper point solutions and AI-driven workflow alternatives.
  • Independent vs. PE Competition: Sam Gerstenzang explains that their model counters private equity (PE) rollups by giving independent operators superior buying power (e.g., Moxy becoming one of the largest buyers of Botox in the country) and complete operational tooling.

Talent, Go-To-Market, and Future Horizons

  • Hiring Criteria: Sam Gerstenzang looks for three traits in talent: high agency, high curiosity, and high judgment.
  • Real-World GTM Integration: Go-to-market strategies are deeply integrated into the physical world, such as hosting med spa nurses at shoe-shopping events or opening a training center in LA with TikTok influencers.
  • Future Sectors: Sam Gerstenzang reveals they are actively looking at trends in the restoration space (pipe bursts, climate change insurance claims), fire safety, and animal hospice over Zoom.

Notable Quotes

*"We start one company every few years very deliberately we spend 100% of our focus on building this company... And we often do it in industries that are overlooked, regulated, complex, or just taboo."* — Sam Gerstenzang
*"No nurse is going to vibe code an EMR. Like she cares too much about HIPPA. She cares about her license. And so she's going to continue to be willing to pay for that."* — Sam Gerstenzang
*"Two businesses both spend $10 million on software and they get different results... and I think that's really collapsing like the the variance of software is getting much smaller."* — Sam Gerstenzang

Freedom vs. Safety: Axon CEO Rick Smith on the Future of Policing

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·55:36·69.0K views·2026-11-10
Rick SmithCEO, Axon David FriedbergHost, All-In Podcast
TL;DRAxon CEO Rick Smith joined David Friedberg on the All-In Podcast to discuss the company's 30-year evolution from a garage startup building non-lethal weapons to a $35–40B public cloud and software giant for public safety, while addressing the delicate balance between civil liberties, privacy, and technological policing advancements.
Founding and Early Struggles: - Founded in 1993 in a Tucson garage by 23-year-old Rick Smith and a 73-year-old NASA scientist who invented the original Taser in the 1970s. - Initial target market was consumers (aimed at mothers like Smith's mom for self-defense), but after...

TL;DR

Axon CEO Rick Smith joined David Friedberg on the All-In Podcast to discuss the company's 30-year evolution from a garage startup building non-lethal weapons to a $35–40B public cloud and software giant for public safety, while addressing the delicate balance between civil liberties, privacy, and technological policing advancements.

Company Origins & Financial Survival

  • Founding and Early Struggles:
    • Founded in 1993 in a Tucson garage by 23-year-old Rick Smith and a 73-year-old NASA scientist who invented the original Taser in the 1970s.
    • Initial target market was consumers (aimed at mothers like Smith's mom for self-defense), but after 7 years of struggling with failed product launches and heavy debt, they pivoted to law enforcement in late 1999.
    • Survived the brink of bankruptcy in 1998 when Smith's father and investor Bruce Culver injected their last $500,000 each after the company owed Silicon Valley Bank about $2 million.
  • Going Public and Subsequent Crisis:
    • Went public in late 2000 at a ~$12M valuation during the dot-com crash because investment banks needed non-internet deals with real revenue.
    • In 2004, the stock peaked as the world's number one performing stock on a 1- and 3-year return basis, but crashed from $33 to $7 in 2005 due to an SEC investigation, short-selling attacks, and an internal mole.
    • Faced hundreds of shareholder and plaintiff lawsuits; notably sued their own insurance company to prevent them from settling meritless claims, ultimately dropping to a $100M market cap before rebuilding to today's $35–40B valuation.
  • The Role of Haddy Partovi:
    • College friend and board member Haddy Partovi acted as a part-time CTO for ~5 years during the software transition.
    • Guided Axon away from managing their own data center (which suffered a near-catastrophic outage) onto AWS and Azure, helped hire the right technical team, and pushed to rebrand the company from Taser to Axon to reflect its broader software and hardware footprint.

Body Cameras & Cloud Infrastructure

  • Creation of the Body Cam:
    • Invented out of necessity to defend officers against false allegations and emotionally charged civil lawsuits (e.g., a 2004 Northern California case involving a meth overdose).
    • Faced initial employee betrayal (an engineer built a competing product in the middle of the night using stock options) and initial police pushback who feared "Big Brother" micromanagement.
    • The turning point for widespread adoption occurred following the 2014 shooting of Michael Brown in Ferguson, Missouri.
  • The Cloud Bet:
    • Made a foundational bet on cloud infrastructure to securely manage and scale the massive influx of video data across 18,000+ police departments.
    • Axon currently manages over a million police officers uploading two hours of video daily, handling more video volume than YouTube.

Surveillance State & Privacy Concerns

  • Balancing Safety vs. Freedom:
    • Acknowledges legitimate public concerns regarding the rise of automated license plate readers (ALPRs like Flock) and surveillance technologies, noting that nobody wants to live in a Chinese-style surveillance state.
    • Argues that society cannot revert to 1950s technology since cameras are already ubiquitous; the core issue is establishing rigorous data governance and regulatory guardrails.
  • Regulatory Solutions:
    • Strongly supports federal legislation introduced by Senator Josh Hawley, which implements criminal and civil penalties for police officers who individually misuse technology (e.g., searching an ex-girlfriend).
    • Advocates for technical safeguards from manufacturers requiring viable case codes and multi-key authorization (requiring another officer or administrator sign-off) before running searches.
    • Notes that demand for policing technology is paradoxically highest in underrepresented, poor neighborhoods suffering from violent crime, where residents cheer for police deterrence.

Robotics, Drones, and Future Policing

  • The Skydio Partnership & Autonomous Drones:
    • Axon became the largest investor in Skydio, securing exclusive channel rights into public safety markets.
    • Autonomous docks placed on fire stations allow drones to arrive on a crime scene within seconds—often before 911 operators finish logging calls.
  • Taser-Equipped Drones & School Shootings:
    • Continuing R&D on non-lethal, Taser-capable drones and robots designed to de-escalate active shooter scenarios (like school shootings) without sending human officers into deadly gunfights.
    • In June 2022, members of Axon's AI ethics board resigned in protest over the project; Axon subsequently reconstituted the group into the Equity and Ethics Advisory Council, adding more diverse voices from impacted communities.
  • Future Robotic Guardrails:
    • Emphasizes that policing will remain fundamentally human-driven, with a strict bright line: decisions to use force will always be made by humans.
    • Future robotic systems will feature real-time administrator approvals and multi-key oversight, making them the "least usable technology in the history of policing" to prevent abuse.

Notable Quotes

*"Why is it that like bullets are still a thing in 1993, much less 2026? ... If we had Captain Kirk's phaser, like people would choose not to kill other people cuz they'd have a more civilized way to stop somebody who's threatening them."* — Rick Smith
*"No cop wants to wear a body camera until they've worn one for 90 days and then they will not go on patrol without it."* — Rick Smith
*"The best deterrence to criminal behavior is not long sentences. It's a high probability that you will get caught and prosecuted."* — Rick Smith

Well, first I would say policing is still fundamentally a human job. Any decisions about using force on another person will be made by humans and that's a bright line for us. So even with the Taser drone you and I talked about earlier, part of what we learned from the concern was people are worried about misuse, abuse, how these things will be used. Human beings don't make good decisions under fight-or-flight. So the fact is the government already has a monopoly on the legal use of force and almost every one of those officers has lethal force, and I will tell you if they feel their life is at risk, the thing in their hand will be a gun. By comparison, if those police are going to come into your house, imagine a future where if they do have to do this mission—and again we can debate about under what conditions they should do it—now they're going to be sending in a drone or a robot. So the operator is going to be a team of people, not just one. It's not going to be a person who's in the fight-or-flight response afraid for their own safety, shaking with their finger on the trigger and if they make a mistake, you're going to get hit with a taser or pepper spray or some non-lethal weapon. And oh by the way, the whole thing will be recorded because to even operate these things you have to have camera systems on them. Many police shootings today are not recorded. And so I think it just comes back to how do we... we just need to sort of take a step back and understand how the job is done today and how we can do it in the future that is safer for everybody and then answer legitimate questions about what are the controls in place, things like multi-key authorization. We will work with our customers to push hard that every one of them has an oversight function, so anytime one of these robotic systems is used, that there's independent civilian oversight and review. So I think again this just comes down to it's not an unreasonable concern. These things could be misused. Right. However, we also don't want to... I don't want to live in a world where everybody else has robotics and AI and the police are showing up in a '74 Chevy, you know, with an old radio. Like they're going to need enough tech to keep up with the rest of us and then the laws and regulations and oversight are going to have to evolve to ensure that we are controlling the government as the people and that we've got a good judiciary and good balance of power so that they can't do abusive things without being held accountable. And what's your view on the shifting political landscape, the rise of a DSA political class today that's calling for defunding the police and what do you think happens to communities and cities over the next couple of years if and as more of that voice becomes elected? Well, if you actually defund the police, we will get some social experiments. I can tell you you don't need an experiment to show what would happen. It will be catastrophic, but democracies recover and I'll give you an example. Yeah, I think it was in 1968 the police in Montreal, Canada went on strike for no other reason, it was simple, it was overpay. There was no George Floyd type situation. In fact Steven Pinker the Harvard psychologist who's... Yeah. ...sort of famously both liberal but also very rational. And so I think obviously it's an important mission and important work you do. Congrats on the business, on the progress, on the Skydio investment. Mercor Raises $750M at $20B Valuation | CEO Brendan Foody

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(78.7K)·54:14·2.5K views·2026-11-10
Brendan FoodyCofounder and CEO, Mercor Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery
TL;DRMercor has grown over 5x on a mid-nine-figure base, scaling to more than $5 million in daily payouts and nearly $1 billion historically to over 5 million experts training frontier AI models.
Founded in 2023 by college dropouts Brendan Foody and Adarsh Suri, the company recently raised a $750 million Series D at a $20 billion valuation with backing from Nvidia, Benchmark, General Catalyst, Apollo, XTX Ventures, and Salesforce. - Rapid Growth & Valuation: - Grew...

TL;DR

Mercor has grown over 5x on a mid-nine-figure base, scaling to more than $5 million in daily payouts and nearly $1 billion historically to over 5 million experts training frontier AI models. Founded in 2023 by college dropouts Brendan Foody and Adarsh Suri, the company recently raised a $750 million Series D at a $20 billion valuation with backing from Nvidia, Benchmark, General Catalyst, Apollo, XTX Ventures, and Salesforce.

Company Overview & Scale

  • Rapid Growth & Valuation:
    • Grew over 5x year-over-year on a mid-nine-figure base.
    • Raised a $750M Series D at a $20B valuation (backed by Nvidia, Benchmark, General Catalyst, Apollo, XTX Ventures, Salesforce).
    • Payouts exceed $5 million daily, totaling nearly $1 billion historically (compared to $1 million total 24 months ago).
  • Global Talent Network:
    • Manages a network of over 5 million active experts across software engineering, medicine, law, and other fields.
    • 70% of experts come in through organic referrals.
    • Performed over 7 million automated AI interviews.

Operational Structure: The Three Core Products

  • Talent Platform:
    • Connects enterprises and AI labs with vetted domain experts.
    • Utilizes Stripe Connect on the backend to handle global payouts reliably.
  • Studio (Data Platform):
    • An onboarding platform where experts use AI co-pilots and agentic QC/automated tools to create Reinforcement Learning (RL) environments.
    • RL environments consist of three parts: *Apps* (clones of tools like Google Workspace, Salesforce, Adobe), *Worlds* (the content/context inside apps like messages, docs, sheets), and *Tasks* (prompts and rubrics used to train and grade AI models).
  • AI Project Manager:
    • Autonomous agents that run projects, hire people, determine data quality, set KPIs, and remain accountable for customer outcomes.

Strategic Acquisitions & Ecosystem

  • Recent M&A Activity:
    • Acquired Deep Tune (backed by A16Z) to solve the app-layer bottleneck by securing clones of Salesforce, Adobe, and Google Workspace to build out RL environments.
    • Previously acquired Seople (founded by Robbie and Cat).
  • Key Industry Partnerships:
    • Works with legal AI platforms like Harvey to curate management processes and data shapes for public legal benchmarks like APEX and APEX agents.

Market Thesis & AI Industry Economics

  • The Data & Jevons Paradox:
    • As AI agents make human experts 3x to 5x more productive, demand for human teaching expands exponentially rather than shrinking.
    • 99% of knowledge remains in human heads; humans must measure the frontier and provide feedback/evals so models can learn.
  • Enterprise & Sovereign AI:
    • Every Fortune 500 company will eventually build its own AI intelligence to codify internal competitive advantage rather than sharing proprietary data with frontier labs.
    • Inference Spend Thresholds: Companies spending under $10M on inference are figuring things out; $10M–$100M spend triggers an investment in *evals* (accounting for roughly 10% of budget); $100M+ spend (like Harvey or Casetext) triggers *post-training* their own models.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Brendan Foody predicts that every Fortune 500 company will eventually operate like a "neolab," building its own core data sets, evaluations, and sovereign AI intelligence.
  • Brendan Foody predicts that enterprise inference spend will ramp materially, driving companies to deploy evals at the $10M–$100M spend range and post-training at the $100M+ spend range.

Notable Quotes

"We have no interest in being second place in the market... We have an interest in defining important categories and I think that just requires more of this like first principles thinking." — Brendan Foody
"The largest misconception about the business is that people think about us as a marketplace like Uber where we would just match experts with a job... when actually the experts are one component of the data sets, the RL environments that we deliver to the labs." — Brendan Foody
"Turning on allowing a frontier lab to train on that data would be analogous to just sharing all of it with a competitor. And so they're not going to do that. And I believe that instead they're going to be building their own models, deploying their own agents that codify all of this knowledge." — Brendan Foody

So, I think the largest misconception about the business is that people think about us as a marketplace like Uber where we would just match experts with a job and then take a fee on when actually the experts are one component of the data sets, the RL environments that we deliver to the labs. And so an RL environment includes three parts. The first part is all of the apps, which are like clones of Google Workspace and other popular applications that people would want to teach agents how to use within the economy. The second is the worlds, which is all the content within those apps. So think the messages, the slides, the docs, the sheets, etc. And then the third part is the tasks, which are prompts and rubrics, similar to how a professor would create a rubric to create an essay where the model can attempt the problem and then get a grade on how well it did that it's able to learn from. And the barrier to automating the entire economy is how do we cover the full distribution of all of the RL environments that mirror everything in the distribution of the economy. So cover all of the different apps people use, all of the different worlds, and all of the different tasks. And so ultimately these like RL environments is the end data that we deliver to our customers. And the experts that work to populate the worlds and the tasks are one ingredient, one moat that has been a foundation for how we do that effectively and scalably. Obviously a lot of this is predicated on models, but how has the kind of the advancement of agents coming into the entire AI system changed and maybe increased what you're doing? Yeah, I think that one of the large benefits is that agents have made it much more productive for someone to spend time creating data and training an agent. And so as an example, like say an hour of someone's time would previously get x amount of product of model improvement. Now an hour of their time might get three to five x that because they're able to use an AI copilot to make them more productive and how they build out all of these data sets. But ultimately, what's happened as we've made the experts more productive is that it's been this case of Jevons paradox where the demand just expanded exponentially because ultimately like 99% of knowledge still lives in people's heads. And if we just make it more effective and productive to have humans teaching models, that just increases the amount that people teach models throughout the economy because it ultimately ties back to the principle I mentioned at the beginning, which is that it doesn't make sense for people to do repetitive work. I think it ultimately comes down to your superintelligence timelines because the market for humans teaching models how to do something is bound by the amount of things that humans can do that models can't do across either the digital world or the physical world. And so so long as there are things in the economy that a human can do, and it might be something that is joining a Google meet and having a persuasive conversation or running a scientific experiment in a wet lab or repairing a car, whatever it is, we need humans that measure that frontier. Like the primary way that models improve is that they need some stasis point for what is the place where they're making a mistake? How do they measure that? And then how do they use that as a reward that they can learn from? It's sort of analogous to the way humans learn of imagine if you are in an English class learning how to write better essays and the teacher just says that you need to like grade all your own essays. And like no one else can give you feedback. You just got to grade your own essays, right? It's like pretty difficult to measure what is beyond the frontier of your own capabilities. And that's why we have feedback. There might be some domains where you can learn based on a clear signal. So I wrote It wasn't a published paper, but I the joke I made on Twitter was that I remember I was in this class my freshman year of college and I had to write about like how can the government pass policies that better align incentives in the economy? And the thesis of my research paper was that the largest positive externality in the economy is creating jobs. And the largest way that the government collects tax revenue is by taxing jobs. And for people that like haven't studied economics, if you tax the employee that is functionally the same as just taxing the company, right? It's like a direct disincentive to job creation. And maybe this sort of makes sense for people that are making a billion dollars a year, like I don't care or I don't make that, but I don't care that much if I don't have a bigger tax bill. But the fact that we do this for the bottom half of Americans that are making under 70k a year is like so deeply irrational. The bottom half of Americans constitute 3% of federal government tax revenue. And yet it's a giant burden on them economically as well as all of the time associated with doing this. Well, since we're talking macro, there was another tweet you put out. By the way, that was a tweet. So we'll tag that tweet. But there was another tweet that you put out that AI progress requires three things, compute, algorithms, and data. Why do you think data is mispriced between that stack? I think data is still contrarian is the top thing where for some reason there has been this perception by investors that we will get super intelligence in three years and AI will be better than humans at absolutely everything. And so we won't need humans to then create evals and train models. And the investors have been saying this for the last three years. Like I remember even in our seed round, most of the top firms in Silicon Valley we've always been very fortunate to work with investors we love, but a lot of the top firms in Silicon Valley just thought that the market would dissipate within a couple of years. And I think the thing that everyone has always missed is the case of Jevons Paradox or functionally the lump of labor fallacy, which is just like as we make the experts more efficient, as the experts are able to improve models three, five, 10 times more, that has dramatically increased the amount of demand for people to improve the models. And ultimately, as long as we don't have super intelligence, as long as there are things that humans can do that AI can't do in the economy, we need humans to measure that frontier. And so I think that has been the largest disconnect. And I honestly feel sort of grateful for it in a lot of ways because we've always had lots of access to capital, but it being contrarian reduces the amount of competition in the market. So I'm glad our market is not nearly as competitive as the foundation model business. But I think that it is on the verge of becoming consensus, especially as people see all of the enterprises that are starting to evaluate agents that they're deploying and post training models. And they need to mobilize some combination of their full-time employees and contractors to curate really high quality tasks for evaluation and training of the models that they're building because ultimately so many of these processes that are currently human are going to become agentic. And this is the most efficient and scalable way to distill all of that human knowledge throughout their company into the models. And so that will be a giant transformation that we're just starting the first inning of. Damn, I was going to ask you that. So I I actually I asked Winston for some questions ahead of time and he wanted me to ask about AI data sovereignty. It's going to be so critical. And for value to accrue to every other company in the Fortune 500 and Americans more generally, we need to make sure that all of these companies are able to train their own models and build their own intelligence that codifies their competitive advantage. Like I think there's sometimes this narrative from certain labs when they're fundraising that all of a sudden they're just going to turn on training on the usage data flywheel of all of the enterprise data at the Fortune 500s are going to be fine with this and this is the way that they're going to collect all the environments throughout the economy. Because you have such a unique and advantageous perspective, I mean, you see literally every company and you see how their data's being used and all this kind of stuff, what advice do you have taking into account everything that you just mentioned on inference, what advice do you have to these enterprises as they continue to build up their sovereign AI stack? I would say that the largest thing that enterprises misinterpret is how Frontier models actually learn, where the vast majority of where the compute gets spent on in training is no longer just like read all the documents in the company or learn from inputs and outputs and SFT. Like most of the compute is now going towards RL where the model will attempt the problem many times and then get a score on these rubrics. And RL in the vast majority of cases requires subject matter experts that are able to create these rubrics and tasks to evaluate and train models. And so for any company that is training their own model, they need to build tasks that produce clear eval scores. And then once they feel confident in the eval, then they should scale up the distribution of what they care about to hill climb that eval. So maybe those are the two pieces of advice is first of all build tasks rather than just pretending the model can read everything and learn. And second of all that they should start with the eval because that is the foundation for how every AI lab and successful application layer understands how well they're doing and has a baseline in improving their models that they're training. Nik Storonsky: The most uncompromising banking CEO

Cheeky Pint(33.6K)·51:50·12.3K views·2026-11-10
Nik StoronskyFounder and CEO, Revolut
TL;DRNik Storonsky, Founder and CEO of Revolut, explains how the neobank scaled to 80 million global customers through organic growth, a rigorous "new bets" validation framework, and a subscription-first business model rather than traditional lending.
Autonomous Small Teams: Revolut builds products by assigning smart product owners, designers, and developers to 10-person teams operating on first principles. Rigorous Validation Process: Ideas pass through a multi-step verification pipeline: Unit Economics & Financials:...

TL;DR

Nik Storonsky, Founder and CEO of Revolut, explains how the neobank scaled to 80 million global customers through organic growth, a rigorous "new bets" validation framework, and a subscription-first business model rather than traditional lending.

Product Development & The "New Bets" Framework

  • Autonomous Small Teams: Revolut builds products by assigning smart product owners, designers, and developers to 10-person teams operating on first principles.
  • Rigorous Validation Process: Ideas pass through a multi-step verification pipeline:
    • *Unit Economics & Financials:* Evaluated by an internal team to ensure potential revenue reaches tens of millions against low initial team costs.
    • *Common Sense Review:* Assessed by general managers and leaders who have built previous new bets.
    • *Build Phase:* Approved teams build the product within 9 to 18 months.
  • Cohort-Based Scaling: Launched products are evaluated monthly against historical data from ~100 previous bets:
    • Top 25% Quantile: Scaled up aggressively (teams multiply into departments).
    • Bottom 25%: Sunsets or pivots; teams are redeployed.
    • Customer Detriment: Products with active users are maintained to prevent negative customer impact.
  • Synthetic P&L Attribution: Non-revenue generating engagement features (like *RevPoints*) are assigned indirect gross profit value based on customer churn reduction and incremental spend.

Growth, Distribution, & The Automated Cross-Sell Engine

  • Organic-Led Scale: Revolut operated without serious paid growth budgets for its first 7 years, relying purely on word-of-mouth, PR, and the core multi-currency card with 0 FX fees.
  • Automated Cross-Sell Machine: A machine learning propensity model combined with an LLM account manager autonomously pushes relevant products (such as eSIMs upon landing in a new country) across the user base without manual distribution friction.
  • Demographics & Primary Banking: Revolut has captured over 50% of people up to 35 years old across most European countries; 10% to 20% of monthly active users are salaried accounts.
  • Negative Churn: Retention curves show a "smile" where monthly active user counts recover and grow over 12, 18, and 24 months due to expanding brand awareness.

Financial Architecture & Subscription Strategy

  • Subscription-First Revenue: Unlike banks relying heavily on net interest margin, Revolut optimizes for transactional and subscription revenue due to strict European interchange caps and credit regulations.
  • Low-Risk Balance Sheet: The target state for lending is less than 10% of the balance sheet, with the rest securitized to maintain tech multiples (40x-50x PE) rather than banking multiples (10x PE).
  • Subscription Success: The *Ultra* subscription account (€50/month) scaled into a massive business, operating similarly to an Amex Premium card.

Operations, AI Integration, & Management

  • AI-First Reimagining: AI is embedded across customer support, translations, engineering, and marketing, with ongoing efforts to build an end-to-end AI employee.
  • Quarterly Performance Management: Performance reviews happen every quarter to quickly identify and exit underperformers, using quantitative metrics, roadmaps, deliverables, and cultural assessments.
  • Lawyer & Back-Office Metrics: Even legal teams are quantified using SLAs (e.g., contract processing speed and deficiency counts).
  • CEO Time Allocation: Storonsky spends 95% of his time internally and limits external conferences to 2–3 times a year.

US Expansion & Global Markets

  • US Market Strategy: Revolut is going head-to-head with Amex Platinum and Chase Sapphire with a credit card-led proposition combined with wealth and crypto trading.
  • Regulatory Milestones: Revolut received conditional banking license approval from the OCC in the US, with final approvals expected around next year.
  • Market Selection: Revolut prioritizes expansion markets with sleepy legacy bank incumbents, high margins, and favorable unit economics.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Nik Storonsky predicts that Revolut's AI venture fund will continue to scale after investing in 40 to 50 deals over the last 2 to 3 years.
  • Nik Storonsky targets an IPO in 2026 (or within 2 years, subject to favorable market conditions), following the US launch and expansion into new credit-led markets.
  • Nik Storonsky claims that within 2 to 3 years, traditional KPIs, roadmaps, processes, and hiring frameworks across companies might become entirely redundant due to AI.

Notable Quotes

"Everything new is forgotten old. We haven't built any product which worked, which wasn't an old product built badly by other organizations." — Nik Storonsky
"The credit business in banking... every time you do a loan or credit card, you need to put provisions against it. It's similar to selling options, right? You kind of collect premium for 10 years, and then year number 11, you lose it all." — Nik Storonsky
"I'm not really optimizing on valuation or readiness for IPO. It's more: 'Okay, this is the roadmap that we want to complete. Let's focus on the roadmap.'" — Nik Storonsky
Do you want to go head-to-head, Amex Platinum and Chase Sapphire? Yeah. How will you compete? Nik Storonsky is the co-founder and CEO of Revolut, one of the world's leading neobanks. They started with foreign exchange, but now do everything from bank accounts to business services to mobile plans. And they're expanding to the United States. Cheers. Cheers. As you look back on it, how did Revolut get to scale? What worked and caused growth? I mean, to be honest here, I was never optimizing to get scale quickly. I was just working on the product and then solving problems end-to-end. Initially when I started, we had probably 1–10 customers per day. Then it went to 100 customers, then to 500, and then to 1,000. Now we have about 100,000 customers joining every day. I was never trying to optimize to get as many customers as possible. I was more focused on building product, gaining licenses, and so on. But you presumably thought a lot about distribution dynamics and how to get customers to refer other customers and things like that. Like, kind of the growth, consumer growth side of things? Yeah, we actually started doing growth, I think, 2022. First time when we started having serious budgets on growth. But you didn't for the first seven years of— No, we didn't. It was purely organic, yes. And that's when we built all the models, paybacks or TVs per customer, and then also built a referral marketing product as well. But the first seven years was just organic, building financial products you thought were good? Yeah. How did people discover them? Word of mouth. Plus we've done, obviously, PR as well. What were the best products from an acquisition point of view? Well, best product was initially the first product that we launched. It was a multi-currency card with zero FX fees. It's interesting that both you and Wise were able to get to scale based on consumers' frustrations with FX fees. The frustration with the incumbents in that product was so high that it has birthed multiple successful fintechs. Probably. If you look at banks, majority of products, they're actually very bad, right? It's not only kind of multi-currency transfers, but also credit, business credit, business products, almost every single product is really bad. I mean, acquiring used to be the bank, right? I think it wasn't about multi-currency transfers or card. It was more that we picked up a certain product, and we made it so much better. It could have been acquiring that you've done. It could have been maybe credit as well, wealth and trading. Robinhood did it. So, you think there's nothing special about starting with multi-currency cards. You think you could have, in another universe, started with checking accounts— Yeah. I think as soon as you build a much better product compared to banks, which is also much cheaper, then it's pretty easy. What was the second product that worked? When did you prove to yourself that this wasn't a fluke, and that you could repeatedly build financial products? Well, business accounts. We built business accounts. The offering wasn't about multi-currency cards anymore, it was a digital bank account. Without any marketing, we first started probably with 10 business customers a day. Now we're onboarding maybe 1,500 business customers a day. And then only... I would say, 30% of it is paid marketing. The rest is still organic. One of the things that, looking at Silicon Valley... Successful product companies tells you is there are many, many paths to heaven. There are many ways to build a successful product organization. And you have Apple that famously doesn't do any focus group testing. They just build the products that they know in their heart of hearts are right in this very functional organization. And then you have... Google or Facebook develop products in totally different ways. What is the Revolut system for building products? We just separate a small team of 10 people. Smart people. Smart product owners. Good designers. Good developers. We just build what makes sense from a first-principles point of view. It can be either replicating an existing product but building it so much better, or trying new products which we think make sense. But to be honest, everything new is forgotten old, right? We haven't built any product which worked, which wasn't an old product built badly by other organizations. We have, internally, a 'new bets' process when everyone in the company can raise an idea, and then it goes through validation. Then if the idea is validated, then it goes to... We'll call it a committee of people who've done it before, and then they decide whether to fund the idea or not. What's validation? First, I think it goes to the team which validates unit economics, financials, and so on. Whether it makes sense. In the majority of cases it makes sense, because the cost of the team is pretty low compared to if the idea is useful for customers. They pay for it, you can generate 10s of millions of dollars, right, compared to the cost for the team, which is small. Then it goes to the people who have built new bets before, and then general managers. They also validate it from, again, a purely common sense perspective. If everything is approved, then we separate a small team. We hire people there, additional people. Then they build a product within... 9–18 months? And then we look at the growth of the product. If there is a lot of growth, then we scale up the team. One team becomes two teams, three teams, four teams, department, and so on. For the bets which don't grow, we sunset them. For bets that grow but not very fast, we just leave them alone. I'm surprised that ideas would need to be validated economically, because you're distributing products across millions of customers. The other thing I find interesting about Revolut's business is you're a bank whose primary revenue source is not net interest margin, which is obviously unlike other banks. The answer is pretty simple. Because we started in the UK and Europe. The UK and Europe conditions are very tough for startups. Interchange is very low, plus credit is extremely regulated. You cannot really charge customers a very high interest rate, same as you can do in Mexico or Brazil, for example. So, for example, you'll have 10, 20, 30 startups starting in financial services, and then the majority will die, right? We were forced to optimize our transactional business, and to effectively make it profitable. But in Europe, it's not possible. But aren't you developing a balance sheet? Because you have one side of it, which is the deposits. You could build the other side of it with loans. Yeah, but before we got deposits, we couldn't build a credit business. Now we're doing credit business. Now, now, yes, now, yes, we— Will you build a traditional bank credit business where you have the deposit side and you have the loan side? Or will it look atypical in some way? Well, I think our business model, and then my personal opinion, is we want to have the least amount of risk. Even if we lend, it won't be a large portion of the balance sheet. At the moment, target state is less than 10%, and then the rest we would securitize. Why? Well. First, the simpler the model, the better from a regulatory point of view. Plus, again, if we want to be a tech business, we would rather be in the subscription business. As soon as you go into the lending business, your multiples become similar to banking multiples. If you look at tech business multiple, you can usually get like 40-50 PE. In the banking business, you'll be lucky if you get 10 PE. But is that because... I'm always skeptical of multiples-based arguments because, ultimately, the business is good or it isn't. And, you can educate investors if the business is in fact, good. When we started Stripe, people thought payments businesses were horrendous and deserving of low multiples, and everything like that. Over time, they came to appreciate payments businesses more. But, if we had been fixated on the multiple that people ascribed to the business, we might have been scared off this path. I think you just need to decide: "Is this lower quality or higher quality as a profit pool?" And so, do you think it's actually a lower quality profit pool, or investors just have a weird hang-up? I think it's more the business itself. If you look at the credit business in banking, general credit business—every time you do a loan or credit card, you need to put provisions against it. In reality, the amount of these provisions is highly dependent on the models that you use, on economic conditions. Because in reality you don't know... In three, or four, or five years, will you receive the loan back, right? You kind of model it. On average you hope to receive money back, but really things can change, right? This uncertainty is being priced in by investors and generally by lower multiples. Because the bank can produce a lot of money now from the credit portfolio. But look, in 2-3 years' time unemployment goes from 3% to 6%. Yep. And all profit is gone, right? Plus they're losing money. So I think this strategy— Okay. You think, fundamentally, making your money via credit is this very cyclical profit stream that can be entirely wiped out in a downturn. It's very sensitive to you doing the math right. Product keynote | How AI is changing SaaS products, pricing, and growth

Stripe(121.0K)·25:07·564 views·2026-11-10
Neetika BansalProduct Leader, Stripe Aakash SahneyProduct Leader, Stripe
TL;DRStripe’s SaaS Platform Summit 2026 highlighted that SaaS platforms are thriving—facilitating over $69 billion in annual payments volume—by optimizing payments through AI tools like Stripe Console, expanding into embedded finance to boost lifetime value by 40%, and adopting hybrid pricing models for AI products via Metronome.
Massive Scale and Volume: Stripe serves over 20,000 platforms globally that help 12 million businesses accept payments. Platforms in the conference room alone facilitate over $69 billion in annual payments volume ($2,200 per second). 767 platforms passed $100M in...

TL;DR

Stripe’s SaaS Platform Summit 2026 highlighted that SaaS platforms are thriving—facilitating over $69 billion in annual payments volume—by optimizing payments through AI tools like Stripe Console, expanding into embedded finance to boost lifetime value by 40%, and adopting hybrid pricing models for AI products via Metronome.

1. State of the SaaS Platform Economy

  • Massive Scale and Volume:
    • Stripe serves over 20,000 platforms globally that help 12 million businesses accept payments.
    • Platforms in the conference room alone facilitate over $69 billion in annual payments volume ($2,200 per second).
    • 767 platforms passed $100M in annual payment volume this year, and 151 reached over $1 billion.
  • Startup Growth Acceleration:
    • New platform businesses on Stripe are up 200% year-over-year.
    • New platforms are reaching $1 million in payments volume faster than any previous cohort.
  • The SaaS Index Reality:
    • The Stripe SaaS Index (excluding pure AI software companies) shows overall SaaS revenues are strong and growth is accelerating, debunking headlines about a "SaaS apocalypse."
  • Top Performing Optimization Strategies:
    • *Aesthetic Record* (med-SaaS and dermatology platform) joined the $1M club by adding BNPL, optimizing checkout rates, and making surgical improvements that doubled their payments volume.
    • *Squarespace* became an end-to-end financial home by offering financial accounts, instant payouts, and easy access to financing.
    • *Genius AI* (stylist platform) bundled three AI products (marketing assistant, receptionist, growth analyst) to get agent-ready and monetize AI-powered tools.

2. AI-Powered Payments Optimization & Pricing Flexibility

  • Platform Growth Studio & Stripe Console:
    • AI-Generated Insights: Console acts as an agentic payments expert built directly into the Stripe dashboard, proactively raising issues (e.g., volume rising 7.9% while take rate fell 6.5 basis points).
    • Automated Root-Cause Analysis: Console investigates payment mix, methods, geographies, and network costs; in a demo case, it traced a margin decline to a single connected account (*Northeast Yoga*) due to an increase in average transaction size.
    • Network Benchmarking: Compares platform performance against Stripe network medians (e.g., comparing Northeast Yoga's 271 bps take rate to a platform median of 400 bps and a Stripe vertical median of 290 bps).
    • Ecosystem Data Scale: Platforms add 35,000 new merchants and process 67 million transactions daily.
    • Early Adoption & Expansion: Private preview users include *Set* (sized opportunity for in-person payments), *Daysmart* (spotted underpriced card volume), and *Circle* (resolved support tickets without engineering hours). Console is rolling out globally in the next few weeks.
    • Developer Access: Data teams can access this intelligence via Stripe Sigma, Stripe data pipeline, and Stripe's MCP for external agents like GPT.
  • Granular Pricing Controls:
    • Funded Discounts: Platforms can choose individual merchants, give custom discounts, and fund the difference from their revenue share.
    • Pass-Through vs. Absorbed Fees: Platforms owning their own pricing can configure fee-by-fee costs (e.g., *TSD Mobility* charges core processing fees like per authorization and network fees; *CAB9* charges for value-added services like 3D secure lookup) using the platform pricing tool.

3. Going Beyond Payments: Embedded Finance Upgrades

  • Financial Home Impact:
    • Platforms embedding multiple financial services see a 40% increase in customer lifetime value.
    • Platforms embedding multiple financial products see 49% faster revenue growth than those offering payments alone.
  • Stripe Treasury Updates:
    • Demand Deposit Accounts (DDAs): Launching real, FDIC-insured bank accounts in the US via partner bank Fifth Third.
    • New Banking Features: Customers can accept/send checks (physical checkbooks launching early next year), load cash at over 60,000 retail locations (Walgreens, CVS), receive monthly statements, and earn cash rewards on balances.
    • Federal Reserve Connection: Direct connection enables same-day ACH payments hours closer to end-of-day cutoff, launching in two weeks.
    • Direct Treasury Deposits: Customers can receive payments directly into their Treasury balance without transferring from a separate payments balance.
  • Stripe Issuing:
    • Platforms like *Shopify*, *Lope*, and *Give Butter* have distributed over 400 million cards via Stripe.
    • New features include offering rewards on card spend without running a separate card business.
  • Stripe Capital Expansion:
    • Growth Impact: Businesses accepting capital offers on Stripe grew 27 percentage points faster over two years than those that didn't.
    • Momentum: The number of platforms distributing capital with Stripe grew 89% year-over-year.
    • Notable Users: *Brightwheel* (preschools), *Jane* (health practitioners), and *FedEx* (shipping tens of thousands of SMBs).
    • Acquisition: Stripe agreed to acquire *Paraffin* (which powers platforms like *Gusto*, *Jobber*, and *MindBody*) to expand credit products.
    • Future Capital Roadmap: Building capital lines of credit, shorter-duration financing (draw-down as needed), context-specific capital (pre-funding marketing, advancing invoices, covering payroll), and expanding eligibility to merchants with zero historical Stripe volume.

4. Monetizing AI Products & Agentic Workflows

  • Agentic Commerce Explosion:
    • Agent volume processed on Stripe grew 21x in September alone following launches of *Muse* and *Instinct*.
    • Use cases span hotels, doctor appointments, concert tickets, and philanthropy (e.g., *Donor Box* accepted the world's first agentic donation in September).
  • Stripe Checkout (Agent-Ready):
    • Uses Model Context Protocol (MCP) to let verified agents land on pages and check out.
    • Trusted agents complete transactions 98% of the time and 39% faster than prior Checkout versions.
    • Early adopters include *Woo*, *Thinkific*, and *Simple Practice*.
  • Hybrid & Usage-Based Pricing via Metronome:
    • The Challenge: Platforms like *HubSpot* (launching AI agent hubs for CRM and marketing) need to monetize AI without destroying seat-based pricing models.
    • Metronome Acquisition: Acquired by Stripe earlier this year to simplify hybrid usage-based and outcome-based pricing.
    • Platform Credits: Allows mixing seat-based pricing with credit purchases (e.g., AI receptionist consumes 5 credits; automatic marketing campaign consumes 40 credits).
    • User Control & Visibility: Includes product dashboard widgets showing remaining credits, usage tracking, and automatic limits.
    • Frictionless Top-Ups: Offers scaling credit discounts and pre-configured automatic recharges for heavy users.
    • Enterprise Adoption: Used in production by *HubSpot*, *Notion*, and *C1*.

Russia-Ukraine War: Putin, Zelenskyy, Drones and the Future of Warfare | Lex Fridman Podcast #504

Lex Fridman(5.1M)·173:49·466.0K views·2026-11-10
Lex FridmanHost, Lex Fridman Podcast Preston StewartMilitary Analyst Michael KofmanMilitary Analyst
TL;DRThe Russia-Ukraine war has evolved into a grueling attritional conflict defined by a massive saturation of drones, electronic warfare, and trench warfare, with neither side currently possessing the decisive military means to achieve maximalist political goals.
Frontline Dynamics and Contested Zones: The main fighting is concentrated in eastern and southeastern Ukraine (Donbas, Luhansk, Donetsk, Zaporizhzhia, and Kherson). The battlefield features a vast 15-kilometer "gray zone" or kill zone where traditional lines are sparse...

TL;DR

The Russia-Ukraine war has evolved into a grueling attritional conflict defined by a massive saturation of drones, electronic warfare, and trench warfare, with neither side currently possessing the decisive military means to achieve maximalist political goals.

Strategic Overview and Frontline Realities

  • Frontline Dynamics and Contested Zones:
    • The main fighting is concentrated in eastern and southeastern Ukraine (Donbas, Luhansk, Donetsk, Zaporizhzhia, and Kherson).
    • The battlefield features a vast 15-kilometer "gray zone" or kill zone where traditional lines are sparse and defended primarily through prepared obstacles, minefields, and dense drone coverage.
    • Russian Infiltration Tactics: Michael Kofman noted that Russia relies heavily on small-scale, highly costly infantry infiltrations using motorcycles, ATVs, and small groups to bypass Ukrainian sensor nets, accepting 90% to 95% casualty rates to slowly chew through terrain.
    • Ukrainian Counter-Operations: Michael Kofman highlighted that Ukraine has conducted successful localized counter-offensives (e.g., in Zaporizhzhia and around Liman) using coordinated drone-mechanized assaults to liberate territory and erase prior Russian gains.
  • The Drone and Tech Revolution:
    • FPV and Recon Drones: Preston Stewart and Michael Kofman pointed out that FPV usage reaches 8,000 to 10,000 drones per day per side, though it actually takes 30 to 40 FPV hits to destroy a well-armored tank.
    • Fiber Optic Drones: Both experts noted the emergence of fiber-optic-spooling drones (ranging up to 50 kilometers) which bypass electronic jamming entirely, though they represent less than 15% of tactical strike drones.
    • The Human Element: Michael Kofman emphasized that despite hype around AI and autonomy, this remains a deeply manpower-intensive, human-centric war requiring extensive pilot, navigator, and technician support.
  • Strategic Bombardment and Civilian Infrastructure:
    • Michael Kofman detailed Russia's expansive strike campaign, which has evolved from cruise and ballistic missiles to jet-powered one-way attack drones targeting energy grids, railways, warehouses, schools, medical facilities, and shopping malls.

Historical Milestones and Lessons Learned

  • The 2022 Invasion and Initial Miscalculations:
    • Michael Kofman explained that Russia launched a failed 30-day coup d'état / quick takeover attempt based on flawed intelligence assumptions that troops would be greeted as liberators.
    • Preston Stewart contrasted the rigid, top-down Soviet/Russian command structure with Ukraine's decentralized Western-style military culture, which empowers junior leaders and soldiers to innovate from the bottom up.
  • The Battle of Bakhmut ("Bakhmut Syndrome"):
    • Michael Kofman analyzed the brutal 2022–2023 siege of Bakhmut, where Wagner convicts were used as expendable assault forces by Russia while Ukraine mounted a politically freighted defense ("Fortress Bakhmut") that ultimately sapped experienced troops.
  • Prigozhin’s Mutiny (June 2023):
    • Michael Kofman and Preston Stewart discussed Yevgeny Prigozhin’s armed march on Rostov and Moscow, highlighting how the mutiny exposed deep friction between regular military leadership (Shoigu/Gerasimov) and Wagner, culminating in Prigozhin's fatal plane crash in August 2023 and the subsequent "Wagnerization" of regular Russian tactical doctrine.

Information Distortion and Military Culture

  • Battlefield Falsification:
    • Preston Stewart and Michael Kofman exposed a pervasive culture of reporting false advances in the Russian military—sometimes using AI-generated or single-soldier flag-waving videos—which tricks senior leadership (including Putin) into believing territory is secured when it remains heavily contested gray zone.
  • Commanders' Dilemma:
    • Michael Kofman noted that Russian and Ukrainian commanders often face immense political pressure to hold untenable positions, creating a "garbage in, garbage out" planning cycle when false metrics travel up the chain of command.

Economic Sustainability, Manpower, and Geopolitics

  • Resource and Economic Strains:
    • Michael Kofman stated that Russia is allocating 44% of its government budget (9-10% of GDP) to the war, consuming its economy over time, while relying heavily on external support from North Korea (which supplied up to 40% of artillery ammunition at points), China, and Iran.
    • Preston Stewart and Michael Kofman noted that Ukraine remains critically dependent on Western intelligence, financial support, and European funding.
  • US Policy and Geopolitical Missteps:
    • Preston Stewart and Michael Kofman criticized recent U.S. administration approaches for prematurely shifting away from a leadership role to a perceived "neutral" arbiter, thereby losing leverage, straining European alliances, and failing to compel Russia toward realistic negotiations.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Michael Kofman predicts: Russia possesses sufficient economic and material resources to continue prosecuting the war into 2027.
  • Michael Kofman predicts: Ukraine will face severe ongoing uncertainty and population displacement during the winter months due to continued Russian strategic bombardment of civilian and energy infrastructure.

Notable Quotes

"The future's already here. It's not evenly distributed." — Michael Kofman (quoting William Gibson)
"A company that mechanized company that goes on the attack now expects to take about close to 300 hits in that assault... The sheer saturation numbers of drones out there is very difficult to overstate." — Michael Kofman
"It's like a sickness we can't kick. For all the ways we've gotten better as people, as humans throughout our evolution, the one thing we keep coming back to is we kill each other over disputes..." — Preston Stewart
