It sets expectations among military and political leaders for who has greater bargaining power, who has leverage, who is succeeding on the battlefield, provides information to the negotiations between the two sides as well, right? So to some extent, we can't overly fixate on the map, but the map is important. And you can see who is adapting, who is not adapting and actually is increasingly suffering greater casualties and battlefield reversals, which in this case, 2026 is Russia. Let's take the operational level. And first, I'll say this is a thorny subject in military circles because I know plenty of people military side that don't think there's a discrete operational level of war. They think that there's operational art, but there isn't such thing as a separate operational level of war. We should not get into that discussion here. - Oh, so you're saying that some people listening to this who are very angry hearing you even say operational like it's this discrete layer. - I would not say they were angry, but I can imagine a host of army majors getting ready to debate this as I bring the topic up. - Okay. Okay. - So let's just stop it and say, look, there are different ways of looking at operational level of war, but there is such a thing as operations and the conduct of operations, right? Which is important. And this is much more the organization of intelligence, logistics, fire support, the coordination of different units in a coherent action with a clear objective, right? And ideally, the operations then link, what you're trying to achieve in terms of the objectives of the operations with the political goals of the actual war, right? Ideally, the military strategy takes the operations and connects them to the political goals of the war. So you're not just conducting successful military operations, but they have a clear purpose in terms of the political objectives you're trying to achieve. And operations are things that are phased, they are planned, they involve coordinating a whole host of units, but they have a clear objective behind them. They have a sequencing. You know, they've thought through how they're going to set the conditions, how the breaching force is going on, who's the second echelon. If you meet with success, who's going to exploit that success? If you don't meet with success, what are the different alternative courses of actions you're going to take? And then ultimately, how are you going to consult, right? So it has a lot of moving pieces. This is what staffs usually plan, and often they're looking at bigger size formations. If we were talking earlier about infantrymen, squads, platoons, companies. This is more at the size of armies, you know, or corps. And then above that, we have military strategy. It's, at its core level, it's a theory of success, right? There's some coherence to the logic of what you're doing, why you're doing it. There are choices being made. It reveals itself often best actually in what you're choosing not to do rather than what you're choosing to do. That's often the most interesting part for me as an analyst. There's some sequencing involved. You've thought through the sequencing of actions. And ideally, it links the actual operations you're doing and planning with the political goals of the war that you receive from your political leaders, right? You know, Preston, you want to add a bit more to that. But military strategy to me is always one of the more interesting topics, at least intellectually. It's... And it's often kind of debated, you know, what, to what ex- to what extent, what is the military strategy, how you understand the military strategy. But this level is important too. - So I always wonder at the, at every layer but the strategy layer, how much does strategy look like Pinky and the Brain, there's that cartoon where, you know, what are we going to do today? Is we're going to take over the world. You kind of assume everything goes flawlessly and it's kind of this dramatic picture of success versus how much planning is there for all the different contingencies, levels of including complete failure. It's like how much of the different trajectories are considered? - Strategy in theory, first, it's a plan that you're not going to change quickly or often because otherwise it's not much of a strategy. But any good strategy must have core planning assumptions, and if those assumptions are proven wrong, you should expect to adjust. Actually, a lot of times strategy goes poorly is when people are trying to execute a strategy even though their assumptions are clearly wrong. I can think of some wars we've been in that looked a bit like that. Second, the strategy is iterative, so it's interacting with the strategy of your opponent, right? And so ideally, you're iterating, you're looking at what your opponent's strategy is, how your strategy is performing against what they're trying to do, and then you're changing it over time, right? But unlike tactics which change quickly and have to keep pace with technology, and tactics inform strategy. If strategy is divorced from tactics, then it's a great staff planning exercise. It may be very well written, but the force can't execute that strategy, right? And alternatively, if you just have tactics, you're just fighting battles, then you don't have a campaign, okay? You have maybe good ways of fighting a war, but you haven't thought through how you're going to win it. You don't have a campaign thought through. It's not obvious how the battles you're fighting are going to aggregate to military success. - Maybe we could take, given everything you laid out now as a case study, the invasion of 2022, February 24th. What were the failures and what were the effective decisions on the Russian side and the Ukrainian side in the early days, in the early months of the war? Maybe, Mike, you could also talk about what you predicted and what turned out to be a correct prediction and what turned out to be not a correct prediction. - Yeah, absolutely. So first, I think amongst community of military analysts and folks who were looking at the situation in 2021, I was definitely one of the people that thought Russia was preparing a full-scale military invasion of Ukraine. And even though we weren't certain, and I wasn't certain of exactly how they would do it or what it would look like, so there were definitely gaps in information. It's rare where you have perfect knowledge of the adversary's plans. Although the U.S. intelligence community had great knowledge of Russia's plans and were releasing that information throughout the Russian military buildup, it was a very difficult time because first, many folks did not want to believe that what they were seeing on the ground, the Russian military buildup and the rhetoric leading up to it, was truly a buildup to a full-scale invasion. In fact, I learned as an analyst that even if all the pieces kind of look right, people naturally want to believe it's anything other than that. This is a very inconvenient truth. And there were a host of countries and allies the United States had that had a different interpretation of the evidence, like Germany and others in Europe. And to be frank, Ukrainian political leadership also had real misgivings about the likelihood of a full-scale invasion. People thought if there was going to be a breakout of conflict again, that it would just be narrowly focused on the Donbas region and that much of the Russian buildup was meant to coerce Ukraine. It was meant to be a part of coercive diplomacy or to distract Ukrainian forces. So there were competing interpretations. I want to be clear about this. And people had different contingencies in mind. Some thought it might be just another Russian attempt to seize the Donbas. Some thought it might be something larger trying to capture parts of eastern Ukraine. And some did think it was going to be a Russian attempt to take most of the country. So I think I was amongst the folks that predicted the invasion early on, Russian intentions and the scope of the operation, but not the way they were going to do it. Because there, I think we over-rationalized the way Russian military would actually conduct the operation, not seeing how the military strategy and the plan, the concept of operations, would be tied to Russian political assumptions. And the Russian political assumptions were that Russian intelligence had set the conditions in Ukraine for a speedy takeover. The Russian troops would be greeted as liberators, okay? That they could conduct essentially a coup de main, that they could very rapidly advance into Ukraine, encircle the capital, either capture or isolate Ukrainian political leadership, and that even though there would be an initial phase of combat operations, it would be brief, and that they could get Ukrainian forces to a large extent surrender or isolate them. These are the Russian assumptions. I'm being clear about that. And that then they could quickly move to a stabilization occupation phase. And all that stemmed from the fact that at the end of the day, that Vladimir Putin did not see Ukraine as a real independent country with a separate national identity, and really had not learned or updated his views at all from the real beginning of the war, which was 2014, right? And we know this because he actually wrote many of them in a July article in 2021. If we go back to that, folks who are interested, it was published. And he laid out a whole series of views of how he saw Ukraine, how he saw the sort of ongoing frozen conflict between Russia and Ukraine, and his assumptions were fairly clear. I don't think we did a good enough job of tying those assumptions to thinking through how Russia might actually do this kind of operation. And secondarily, the toughest thing to predict, the interaction between the two forces. Here, it's not a case of overestimating the Russian military, just underestimating the Ukraine military, although I think there's a fair argument to be said that we overestimated how Russian military would perform in this case. We underestimated how the Ukraine military would perform in the initial opening of the war, in part because we knew far less about the Ukraine military. I'm gonna be very frank about that. Most expertise is usually against adversary forces. We don't have nearly as much expertise amongst ally, for allies and partners. That's just the nature of the field and the profession we work in. And secondarily, I think actually more was known about Russian plans to attack than Ukrainian plans to defend. So there's a degree of anchoring bias that information has when you do analysis. But also much was contingent. So Ukrainian forces perform, not only did they perform much better, but Ukrainian volunteers showed up to fight in those early moments. But it was a very chaotic early period of the war, the initial clash and reconciliation of the Russian invasion and the Ukrainian defense. I think one challenge that analysts have and folks who look at analysis is in understanding that military power by itself cannot be abstracted. I often see it abstracted in conversations, a sense the United States is number one and China's number two and Russia's maybe number three or four or five, wherever you, you wanna put it. But there's no such thing. Military power requires a context to express itself, right? So if you're thinking of a potential, let's say, battle between United States and China over Taiwan, you have one scenario in mind, okay? If you're thinking of maybe a war between China and India, you have a different scenario in mind. So this abstraction isn't helpful. It actually really matters what the scenario is because the geography matters, the political objectives matter. They will drive the military strategy and the concept of operations, and that will then heavily decide how the forces will be employed, which is the most important thing here. And so sometimes what happens is you may know something about a military, or you may even know quite a bit about both militaries, but knowing something about militaries is not the same as knowing how the two of them will interact in a particular fight. And that is subject to a lot of individual choices, political choices, military choices, and also a great deal of serendipity and chance, things that are contingent. I'll say this about the opening of the war. As poorly thought through the Russian invasion was, and as unworkable as their assumptions were, you know, in many cases, the Ukrainian defense itself was improvised. And so that first clash was, in my view, contingent on a whole series of choices and early battles that went a certain way. And we'll continue to debate this military history for a long time. You know, I think I and other folks who are interested in military history, we still debate what happened in World War II, what happened in World War I, what happened in the Napoleonic Wars. The reason I bring this up is for a degree of humility, this war is hardly over. We still have a long ways to go to piece together what happened exactly and why. And often we don't have a great understanding of the probability of a timeline, because people naturally assume that whatever timeline they're living in, whatever happened, was the likeliest series of events to take place. They rarely ever assume that they're living in a fortunate version of that timeline. This is what we're trying to accomplish and why, the task and purpose and all of that. We were spread across a lot of small bases in Afghanistan, so you had captains and below, sometimes 24, 25-year-olds, making daily life and death decisions for a lot of people, and we would push that down to 20-year-old staff sergeants or sergeants that were making a decision, that could cost the lives of their men. - So you're saying like a 19-year-old, 20-year-old, if they have a good idea, if they have a good decision, that has power? - Yeah, there's a lot of leadership involved with that, 'cause you can't just grab a random guy and say, "We're gonna let you make whatever decision you want." Where Startups Can Win Beyond OpenAI and Anthropic | HubSpot’s Dharmesh Shah

a16z(308.0K)·59:07·4.9K views·2026-11-10
Joe SchmidtPartner, a16z Dharmesh ShahCofounder and CTO, HubSpot
TL;DRDespite anxieties surrounding frontier AI labs expanding into applications, builders have an unprecedented software opportunity by focusing on narrow markets, solving entire customer problems, and building outside the direct path of the labs.
According to HubSpot co-founder Dharmesh Shah, AI acts as a higher-level abstraction layer and infrastructure—much like cloud computing 15 years ago—making it the best time ever to be a builder. The Best Time to Be a Builder: Dharmesh Shah emphasizes that AI provides a new tool...

TL;DR

Despite anxieties surrounding frontier AI labs expanding into applications, builders have an unprecedented software opportunity by focusing on narrow markets, solving entire customer problems, and building outside the direct path of the labs. According to HubSpot co-founder Dharmesh Shah, AI acts as a higher-level abstraction layer and infrastructure—much like cloud computing 15 years ago—making it the best time ever to be a builder.

Market Context & The Builder's Opportunity

  • The Best Time to Be a Builder: Dharmesh Shah emphasizes that AI provides a new tool set operating at higher levels of abstraction, dramatically accelerating iteration speed and allowing founders to learn lessons in days or weeks rather than years.
  • The Software Abstraction Arc: Software development has historically progressed through increasing levels of abstraction—from machine language and mainframes to 3Gs (C/C++) and 4Gs—with AI now opening a new tool chest and expanding the scope of who can become a builder.
  • The Solopreneur Wave:
      • Record Company Formation: The United States saw 5.6 million new company formations, with expectations of reaching nearly 6 million.
      • One-Person Businesses: Over 70% of new company formations are predicted to remain one-person (solopreneur) companies that are unlikely to hire an employee within the next two years.
      • Uspot: In response, HubSpot launched Uspot (a CRM for one-person businesses, exactly 30 days old at the time of recording), which monetized rapidly via a consumption-based model.

Startup Strategy & Idea Selection

  • Flaws in Rigorous Analysis: Dharmesh Shah warns founders against relying on spreadsheets and US Census data (e.g., calculating "18,000 law firms") to pick ideas, noting that it is almost impossible to know a priori the quality or success probability of an idea.
  • Fast Iteration over Analysis: The only reliable way to test an idea is to launch it quickly; successful businesses rarely resemble their original concepts, meaning the winning strategy is minimizing iteration time and learning cost.
  • Narrowing the Aperture: Founders should avoid starting with a massive TAM and instead focus on a narrow wedge to get to $1 of revenue first, ensuring short iteration loops and cheap learning.
  • Solving the Whole Problem: Drawing from HubSpot's history—starting with a broad suite of marketing applications (CMS, SEO, landing pages, forms) despite conventional advice to do just one thing—founders must solve the whole customer problem to eliminate customer intimidation caused by fragmentation.

HubSpot’s Evolution: From LegalSpot to CRM Platform

  • The Origin Story: Founded as an ERP for small businesses, tested via an MIT business plan competition as "LegalSpot" (focused on law firms), HubSpot quickly realized lawyers were a brutal market to sell into and pivoted to SMB marketing.
  • The 60% Opportunity: An internal pre-IPO survey revealed that while 20% of HubSpot customers used Salesforce and 20% used other CRMs, 60% were not using a CRM at all because existing tools were overwhelming and expensive.
  • The CRM Wedge: HubSpot launched its CRM by removing barriers: making it extremely easy to use for SMBs and making the core version free (subsidized by marketing software), proving the philosophy of creating customer value before extracting it.
  • Ecosystem Philosophy: Platforms must act as "warm-hearted red-blooded capitalists" who build trust by sharing white-space boundaries with developers and reducing "time to joy" (how fast a developer can make their first dollar using HubSpot APIs).

Navigating the AI Landscape & Staying Off the "Yellow Brick Road"

  • Avoiding the Labs' Path: Joe Schmidt's "Yellow Brick Road" essay highlights the danger of competing directly where frontier AI labs are racing; founders must stay off that path to avoid being crushed by well-funded giants.
  • Incentive and Structural Constraints: Frontier labs (OpenAI, Anthropic) are focused on selling inference and tokens (via consumer chat apps or direct Fortune 500 sales like JPMorgan), making it economically and strategically unlikely that Sam Altman or others will target niche vertical SaaS applications.
  • AI as Infrastructure: Treating AI models like AWS 15 years ago means models are trending toward fungibility and pricing commoditization (aided by open source and model routers), removing the fear of single-supplier monopolies.
  • Interface vs. Underlying Value: Like Uber Eats (where the app interface is trivial compared to logistics and restaurant networks) or early web browsers, AI chat interfaces are just new consumption browsers; the true durable value lies in workflows, customer context, and the systems where work actually happens.

Architectural Evolution & Internal Innovation

  • System of Record vs. System of Context: While relational databases remain essential for structured data and deterministic queries (e.g., pulling revenue from Ireland), HubSpot is building a "context graph" (second brain) to handle unstructured data, logs, and messages.
  • Agentic Code Rewrites: Using agentic coding and automated evaluation batteries (evals), engineering teams can test alternative architectural decisions and run hypothetical code rewrites (e.g., testing language or framework changes) for minimal cost ($200,000 in tokens).
  • HubSpot Next: An internal innovation lab operating as a "startup within a startup" with zero corporate baggage, allowing rapid experimentation on new product categories, technologies, and pricing models (such as Uspot).

Notable Quotes

"The actual thing that’s valuable is the work that’s happening. The interface is a means to an end." — Dharmesh Shah
"Never own a restaurant. Number two is don't ever be a landlord and charge rent. And number three is never compete with Sam Altman." — Dharmesh Shah
"Create customer value before you try to extract it." — Dharmesh Shah
"Anything that can be solved with a structured query deterministically, solve with a structured query deterministically. Don't use inference to solve something that a query can solve." — Dharmesh Shah

Yeah, I'll give you a slightly geeky response because I'm a geeky guy. Having been in the software industry since prehistoric ages 150 years ago, the story of software development has always been this kind of increasing levels of abstraction. When I started, we still had machine language, we were running on mainframes and things like that, then we had 3GLs, we had C and C++, and we had 4GLs. What we've done is said, okay, can we get to whatever manifestation you have in your head of whatever problem you're trying to solve, can we make that easier for you to do? Back then it was really, really hard, and then it got progressively easier. We've had some jumps along the way in terms of nonlinear increases in the level of abstraction, but at its base, this is the thing that excites me about AI. It gives us a new toolset to work at yet higher levels of abstraction. That's the thing that's sort of changed. What it does is we're just putting more tools in the tool chest of builders and we're expanding the scope of who can be a builder, which is awesome too. But a lot of it is still the same. We're still dealing with similar issues, and it's just nice to be able to work at a higher level of abstraction. One of the reasons I think this is a great time—it's always a good time to do a startup, but it's a great time to do a startup—is that because we're working at such high levels of abstraction, AI lets you iterate so much faster. Oh yeah. So you can learn the lessons that might have taken you six months, a year, or two years to do. It's like, okay, well, just try it, build something, get something out there, test it with actual users and customers, and see what happens. That just wasn't possible before. Oh my gosh. The only real way to test an idea or know if an idea is good is to actually test it. Everyone always wants to end up being the HubSpot for whatever use case. Maybe talk a little bit about how you navigated that product journey and created the right loops to get to more of a platform from this initial wedge. We started off as a suite of marketing applications. To answer your first question, this was not at time t equals zero, we think we're going to be a CRM platform someday. That was not in any of our PowerPoint decks. As the company evolved, we sort of—and this may be a little bit idiosyncratic to HubSpot, but I don't think so, the pattern still exists even outside of a CRM—you sort of have the need for product number two, the second act however one phrases it. We sort of hit that. A couple things happened simultaneously. We were in the marketing software business, and one of the things we recognized is that being in the marketing software business was very, very close to sales and CRM. That was the adjacent category, so that made logical sense. The other thing that we learned once we had a bunch of customers is that we did a survey of our customers—this is maybe a couple years before the IPO, maybe five, six years into the company—just to see which CRM they were using. We discovered that 20% were using Salesforce.com, another 20% were using some other CRM, but 60% were not using a CRM at all. That was insane to us. You're using HubSpot software, you're paying us money, so these are not random companies, these are people paying us money to generate leads. What's happening to those leads if they're not where you put them? The thing that we settled upon was, okay, well, these companies should be using a CRM. My co-founder and I are very weirdly simple-minded. Why are they not using a CRM? The categories exist, there are great companies with great products out there. We came down to, oh, it's overwhelming. They're just intimidated by the idea of a CRM. It's too expensive, they think it's going to cost too much to get going with a CRM. We made the decision that it was a nice adjacent thing, we needed a second product anyway, there was a strategic value to saying over the fullness of time the existing CRM players are likely going to get into the marketing space, so even if we wanted to stay in the marketing software category, it's unlikely that we're going to be able to do that forever. All those things converged around the same time period. When we launched the CRM, we sort of addressed both of those issues. We're going to build a CRM, and we're going to address the 60% for those people that have not picked a CRM yet. Why have they not done it? Too hard. Let's make the easiest CRM that's ever been conceived at that point in time. We were focused on SMB, so that was a core strength anyway. We're going to take "it's too expensive" off the table because we're going to make it free, not freemium, free. This has been sort of the philosophy at HubSpot: create customer value before you try to extract it. If you can just get out there with something that's useful—and we could obviously subsidize it with the marketing software business that we already had—that was the idea. We've removed the obstacles, let's get out there, and as we iterated we learned that in order to win in the CRM space it needed to be a platform. CRM is almost by definition now, and even then, are platforms. That was a journey, going from product one to product two, expanding out into a strategic adjacent thing. This is the other thing I think that carries over to the entrepreneurs listening: do what you can to solve the whole customer problem, however you define it, whatever circle you draw around it. What's the actual problem we're trying to solve? Even when HubSpot started, we had a very broad base of marketing applications, which goes against every piece of advice you get and that I've given: do one thing really, really well. We did all the things. We had content management and SEO and landing pages and forms, and there were great companies with great products in each of those categories. The reason we chose to do it all is the only way for us to solve the actual problem customers had, which is they're intimidated by this fragmentation. We had to do it all in one offering. We carried that idea with us for 20 years and we still do the same thing now. What's the actual problem we're trying to solve? Well, it's so interesting because I think with hindsight everyone always talks about, from a go-to-market strategy, there's the brownfield approach of if you go after all these people who are on every core system already and you rip and replace it, and hopefully you're in the middle of a cycle like this where everyone's willing to revisit that, or you go after greenfield and you try to win all these new businesses who have never adopted software before. The standard thinking on HubSpot is, oh well, it was just very thoughtful from the beginning, let's go after the greenfield. The reality is, actually no, all of our customers just didn't have it and we built it, and it's totally fine if you do it in that order and you can still be a massive company if you nail it. So interesting. How did you think about building the right ecosystem around that initial offering, right? That's something that HubSpot has done such an amazing job of. What are the key learnings from the early days of building the ecosystem, and now that it's such a different role today where you're able to do a lot of that in different ways? I'd love to hear you talk about that with the advent of AI and how that's impacting the ecosystem as well. I've been a consumer of platforms and ecosystems for most of my professional career, so I know what it's like on both sides. This is no disparagement to any of the existing platforms then or now, but what tends to happen with ecosystems and platforms is that the platform providers will start looking to attract the developer ecosystem and get people to build apps or build companies that are adding value to the core platform, but over time what ends up happening is that either intentionally or unintentionally they sort of stomp over those developers. They'll close things up, they'll shut down. We saw this—this story is 15 plus years old now—Twitter had this in the early days. The original Twitter was this very open ecosystem, they were a platform, and literally a thousand flowers bloomed back then. Lots of Twitter clients built, lots of people built products and companies on that original set of APIs. It's not exclusive to Twitter, that's just what jumps to mind right now. HubSpot launched our connector to ChatGPT over a year ago, and we have one for Anthropic. One of the questions we get sometimes is, oh, well, doesn't that sort of diminish the long-term value of HubSpot? If everyone's coming in through ChatGPT or through cloud co-work or something like that, isn't that eroding the long-term value? I understand the reasoning, my counter to that is that the interface matters, but it matters to varying degrees. There's a spectrum. Let's say one end of the spectrum—I'm making this up as I go—let's say you look at something like an Uber Eats or a DoorDash. They have very compelling apps, but the app is trivial. What's actually valuable is the network that they have, the partnerships of the restaurants, so a driver can show up, pick up food, and deliver it to the logistics and all of that. That's where the actual value is, and that's the part that's hard to displace. There are other things where the interface is the entirety of the product, that's all there is, if it weren't for the interface there would be no value. Most business applications actually sit more towards the Uber Eats kind of thing, not in the sense that they have network effects like Uber Eats and DoorDash do, but in the sense that the actual thing that is valuable is the work that's happening. The interface is a means to an end. The only reason the interface is important to HubSpot is because marketers and salespeople are trying to get something done or deliver on certain outcomes, and the interface is the way, but in order for them to communicate with a computer—before AI happened, HubSpot's claim to fame was we understand the problem you're trying to do, whatever, and we've actually built the interface that's super simple to help you get there, but that was not the entirety of the value. Now, one can argue where on the spectrum HubSpot falls, but I can guarantee you it does not fall in the "interface has 99% of the value." My argument is that in the same way everything comes back around again, when we saw the original SAS development just moving to the web-based application world, we had the same kind of thing. We had client-server apps running on our desktops back in the day, and then we had this browser thing come along, Internet Explorer and later Mozilla. My software is running inside of a browser, but as it turns out, Google did not own the entire application stack. There were lots and lots of software companies that got built, and the interface became the browser because that was a convenient way to get to the actual product that you needed to get to. The browser was a better way than installing something on 19 million computers. We have a similar thing now—it's different because the AI is doing work, it's doing actual smart things, they can do more things. But if you just look at ChatGPT and cloud co-work, they are the new browsers, this is the way people are consuming value. There will be companies like HubSpot that sort of plug in—this is the thing we saw from DevDay, and we saw it last year and we continue to see it—they're going to increase the richness of what's available within ChatGPT. They launched a marketplace for people to plug in, a plug-in architecture and all those things, which makes complete sense to me. My fundamental philosophy, which I think holds true for most people looking to, at least in the B2B world, is don't overthink the interface, figure out what's the best possible way to achieve the outcomes your customers are looking for, and then the other value that you can bring is around what's the economic best way, what's the lowest latency way, what's the lowest friction way to accomplish those outcomes. Often an LLM is very, very good at lots and lots of things, it's the most brilliant technology we've had in the history of humankind. But one of the things I've gone on record of saying is anything that can be solved with a structured query deterministically, solve with a structured query deterministically. Don't use inference to solve something that a query can solve, it doesn't make any sense. Even if the data were there, if your question is how many customers or how much revenue are we getting from Ireland, you shouldn't have to troll through 8 million Slack messages and emails or whatever, infer the answer. Yes, the data is probably there somewhere, but that should be a database query, that should not be solved through inference and throw a 300 IQ model at it. No, totally. I think there's many companies that are navigating through this as they try to understand their own pricing and consumption ideas here, which could be its own dang podcast I think for that matter. One is, I'm a classically trained computer science software development nerd forever, and one of the things I'm a big believer in in terms of large-scale software applications is around this notion of frameworks and starting with a set of primitives that you sort of evolve over time, because that's the thing that gives you—coming back to compounding and leverage—one of the things I've learned across every company I've done and worked in is that software, if you can get those core primitives right, and then what you end up doing is remixing and recomposing those primitives in novel ways based on how the market evolves and how the product evolves. That part has been true at HubSpot since the beginning. The thing we built out was we had this core operating system or operating framework that we had on which we built the marketing automation things like that. So when we went to product 2, 3, 4, or whatever, most of the value that we needed to bring to market already existed in the form of primitives. You touched on one of the things: the data is no longer just this structured data in a relational data store or whatever, which made sense for the kinds of deterministic queries you're doing. Now we revisit the primitives and say, okay, we still need a structured data store—and I will happily debate this with whoever wants to debate it, there is still room, whether it's relational or some other, there needs to be some persistent mechanism for storing known facts and data, some sort of system of record if you will, alongside this kind of system we think of as a system of context, which is this unstructured data.
